So allow me to try and help you guys in this video. The first thing we need to understand and come to terms with is the reality that the relationship has come to an end. Truly, it is a place which I recommend visiting at least once in your life. Now, to get there, you want to fly to the capital called Mali, and then from landing on that island, you would then either take a boat transfer to your resort, or you would take a seaplane to your resort. Now, there's pros and cons of both. When you take a boat transfer, usually it means it's going to be more convenient. You just walk out of the airport and you pretty much hop onto the boat, and then within 15 to 45 minutes, you can get straight to your island. They tend to be a little bit more frequent and more flexible. With the seaplane, I would say it is a little bit more inconvenient and generally does tend to add a couple more hours to your overall travel time. When you land in the main airport, Mali, you then have to go to another airport around the corner. You usually have to wait a little bit in a lounge or waiting room, and then you board a seaplane to fly to your resort. The seaplanes tend to be not that luxurious. And if you're the type of person that doesn't really care about the whole luxury experience and having an absolutely ginormous room with unnecessary amenities, then don't spend that money. Because at the end of the day, what you want from the Mollies, what a lot of people would want is just to see the beauty of the Mollies and to see the blue water, go for the swims, enjoy the weather, and to see the marine life. So there's loads of resorts that are pretty bog standard, but still very beautiful. Like the island itself is super beautiful. The water is beautiful and it's probably got some pretty decent marine life as well. So that's something to take into consideration. And then on top of that, you've got to also make sure that you go in with the right person. There are maybe some resorts that throw parties in there, but more of a social resort. But for my experience so far, it's very much couples. It's also worth noting that some resorts are obviously going to have more emphasis on the spa, the wellness and the gym facilities. That is just something that I would not want to do. I want to be in as much nature as possible. So I feel like if you do stay on a resort on a manmade island, it might spoil that authentic experience. And then you need to think about the duration of your stay. And of course, for a stay that short, it would make more sense to get a sea boat transfer as it just speeds up the efficiency of everything. If you're going to be staying for a longer period of time, then taking the sea plane makes a little bit more sense. But if you're going to be staying for more than one week, you're probably going to get a little bit bored. There is not a whole lot to do in the Maldives. So if you are going to go for longer than a week, I would maybe split the trip up into two resorts because staying in the same place, the same island is probably going to get repetitive after a couple of days. I think strain is really good for those athletes who are doing more cardio-based activities. So this is something which I have to take with a pinch of salt. I don't think it's necessarily, I think it underestimates just how strenuous my training sessions are. Okay, those workouts, I can only do four to five times per week. And then aside from that, I need to be resting if I want to optimize my recovery and build as much muscle tissue as possible. It also has a sleep planner, which has been very useful, I must admit. This is recommending what times you should go to sleep and what time you should wake up. And this is based off your recent habits of when you've fallen asleep and when you've woken up. For those people who are looking to improve their sleep, you need to understand that the consistency of when you go to bed and when you wake up is going to have a massive impact of how quickly you fall asleep and how good the quality of the sleep is. So if you can try and keep it as consistent as possible when you wake up and fall asleep, that's going to drastically improve your sleep. So this sleeping coach is trying to tell you to at least stick to a decent enough routine to give you the best chance of having a good night's sleep. The health monitor is also really cool. That is a clear signal to me that my body is fighting something, it's going through something. So whenever I do get a notification saying that things are a little bit off, I take it very easy that day. I don't do anything strenuous and I just focus on letting my body recover.  what's up guys and welcome to another video. Today we will be debunking some of the most common myths within the fitness industry at the moment, or at least some of the most common ones which I believe are just getting in the way of people making significant progress. So in no particular order, the ones that stand out the most to me, starting off with number one is that you need to do cardio in order to lose body fat. Okay, the truth of the matter is if you want to lose body fat, there's two ways you can do it. You either do that by consuming fewer calories or you do that by burning more calories on a day-to-day basis. So let me show you what I mean. This is a representation of how many calories an individual would burn on a day-to-day basis and this is where it's coming from. Resting metabolic rate, thermo-effective food, non-exercise activity, thermogenesis and then actual exercise. Okay, so these two things you can't really change, right? There's at least in the short run, you can't change your resting metabolic rate or the thermic effect of food, but these two you can. You can change how much exercise you do and you can change how many calories you burn just moving your body on a day-to-day basis. Now most people will think, okay, I want to burn more calories so the only way for me to do that is to just do more exercise and this usually becomes a little bit problematic because it means either going to the gym more often, which most people don't want to do, or trying to burn more calories in the gym when really when you're in the gym the goal should be to actually just build muscle tissue, not maximize calories burn. Okay, and then going to the gym and just doing cardio just sucks and it's usually boring, but yes ultimately you will burn more calories. Now what you could do in order to burn the same amount of calories is change how you burn it. So you don't necessarily have to do more exercise. You don't have to go to the gym more often or try and burn more calories in the gym. You can just figure out how on a day-to-day basis you can just move your body more. Okay, so this is in the form of just walking around, increasing steps, just bouncing your knee upwards and downwards. Maybe you're taking a call and you're doing steps and walking around the room while doing a call, or maybe you just decide to you know walk to work or just stay on your feet and not sit down all day. Okay, so this isn't necessarily directly doing more exercise, but it is a way of moving your body more so that you burn more calories. The next myth is that you need to be consuming an excessive amount of protein in order to build muscle tissue or even just to maintain it. I for years used to believe and used to swear by hitting the target of 2.2 grams per kilogram of body weight, which when you think about it particularly the heavier you get, that's a hell of a lot of protein and that's a lot of meat, fish, eggs, protein powders which you need to consume in order to hit those targets and it can be stressful when you think oh my god I haven't hit my target so many days. In reality the studies have shown that 1.6 to 1.7 grams per kilogram of body weight is pretty much all you need in order to build muscle tissue. Anything above that isn't really going to accelerate muscle growth at all, so that's good news. It means you can consume more fats, you can consume more carbs which realistically are probably going to enhance your performance and your recovery. Now it is worth saying the older you get protein requirements might be more and if you're in a calorie deficit protein requirements might be a little bit higher. So overall I would say it's probably better to be on the safe side and maybe over consume protein just a little bit as an insurance policy just in case you were in a situation where you did need to consume more protein but for the vast majority of people you just want to look athletic, they want to put on a bit of size, 1.6 to 1.7 grams per kilogram of body weight is going to be absolutely fine. Another huge myth is that you need to be sore after your sessions, you need to experience a pretty significant amount of delayed onset and muscle soreness otherwise this session was just not good enough, you need to try harder next time. Which is not true, in fact you can make progress, lift heavier weights, get bigger and stronger without actually needing to experience the soreness at all. That's actually a good indicator that your programming is on point and you're not doing excessive volume, excessive reps, sets, drop sets and so on. The thing is with soreness, if you experience too much of it basically means it's taking that muscle longer to recover. So it's more time until you can actually train that muscle group once again and on top of that it's just straight up uncomfortable because you feel like you're walking around like a cripple. Nobody likes training legs and not being able to walk properly or sit down five days after the session. Okay so actually doing fewer sets, maybe fewer exercises in a particular session, but still trying to lift as heavy as possible and each week lifting heavier means that the soreness will actually be reduced but you will still be making progress. The next myth is that you need to cut your carbs if you want to reduce body fat percentage. If you're going to go on a cut the best way to do it is just eliminate your carbs. Well that's not entirely true. So let me give you an example of what I mean. This is an individual who's consuming 3,000 calories at the start of a cut and they want to lose body fat but they are convinced that you can only lose body fat by cutting the carbs. What ends up happening by reducing the carbs drastically from their diet, they end up cutting a significant amount of calories from their intake. Now of course this will give you results but often you end up dropping your calories drastically in a very short period of time which is certainly not advised at the start of a cut. Okay they don't increase their fats and they don't increase their proteins, they just cut their carbs. There's a huge deficit and usually what this means is there's a massive drop in performance in the gym, they get weaker, recovery is slower, the muscles are less full and then they end up having these pretty aggressive cravings because of the elimination of carbs and they're not replacing the fats. This is what I would do, a person who is trying to maintain muscle mass and lift as much as possible, stay full, stay satiated. I would just drop my fats a little bit. I would keep my carbs exactly the same, keep protein the same and just drop the fats and you can see we're in a deficit and that is all we need in order to lose body fat. Of course as you want to continue dropping calories I wouldn't recommend eliminating fats because you need the fats. Okay so once you get to a certain point where it doesn't make sense to drop fats any further then you can start dropping the carbs a little bit. I wouldn't recommend dropping protein if anything protein may need to increase a little bit but you shouldn't be eliminating or dropping carbs drastically at the start of a cut. Just over time you want to look at very gradually reducing fats, carbs, allowing yourself to be in a calorie deficit but not an excessive calorie deficit especially to begin with in this example. The next myth is to do with training to failure. A lot of people seem to think if you want to grow you want to build muscle tissue you have to push yourself and trace failure on every single set. This is not true okay for the majority of people, beginners, intermediate lifters and even experienced lifters you don't need to do that. The true indication of progress in building muscle tissue is progressively overloading on a week to week basis. How can you increase the mechanical tension in the exercises which you're doing? The best way to do that is just lift a little bit more each week. The problem with pushing to failure two things. First of all when you push to failure the risk of injury is much higher. This goes for everyone even if you're an advanced lifter. If you just have one slip of concentration the risk of injury is very high. If you're a beginner intermediate lifter you don't have the experience or the mastery of exercise execution so you often can't control the weight and will not have you form and check and end up doing damage okay and injury that's the absolute worst thing for you. The second point is the fatigue and the muscle damage that's caused from going to failure too frequently okay. If you were to push to failure on every single set you're going to be much more fatigued in comparison to somebody who has one or two reps in reserve. So what does that mean? You need longer periods of rest and recovery in between sessions. If you like me find it very hard to not push to failure then what would make sense would be to tape it on the volume okay so do fewer working sets per session per week and that way if you do push to failure this is like seriously pushing to failure then you're not going to be doing as much damage and causing as much fatigue because you're not doing it as often. Let's say for example if you're pushing to failure and doing that in three to four working sets per exercise okay so it is relative is kind of dependent upon the individual and what their idea of failure is but you don't need to do it in order to progress so don't think you know just by literally shaking on those last repetitions borderline letting the weight drop on your face or causing major injury you don't need to do that okay just track your progress the weights you're lifting and the goal each week should be to lift more. Another common myth which people believe is that whenever they're cutting and trying to lose body fat they need to increase the reps that they're doing for each exercise they do in the gym. They think well if I do more repetitions then I'm going to burn more calories and this is what I want to do because obviously I'm losing body fat but this is actually the opposite of what you want to do okay if you are doing more repetitions you cannot lift the same amount of weight for more repetitions what you're actually doing is you're reducing mechanical tension you're going to actually accelerate the amount of muscle tissue which you lose and strength which you lose because of this reduction in weight which you're lifting and of course you combine that with fewer calories it's going to accelerate even further so when you're in the gym focus on preserving as much muscle tissue as possible size strength of course when the calories are in abundance you can focus on really growing
